import PowerStepper from '../../components/PowerStepper.astro';

## The idea in one sentence

Almost every direction gets turned when a matrix acts. A few come back pointing exactly the way
they went in, only longer or shorter, and those few decide everything that happens when you apply
the matrix again and again.

## The directions that survive

Lecture 2 left you with a matrix as a machine: a vector goes in, a moved vector comes out. Now
stop watching the whole plane and watch one arrow at a time. Take

$$
A = \begin{bmatrix} 4 & 1 \\ 2 & 3 \end{bmatrix}
$$

and feed it $(-1, 1)$. Out comes $(-3, 1)$. The arrow got longer, and it also swung around toward
the horizontal. Output and input point different ways. That is the ordinary case, and it is what
almost every arrow does.

Now feed the same machine $(1, 1)$. Out comes $(5, 5)$: the same direction, five times as long.
Feed it $(1, -2)$. Out comes $(2, -4)$: the same direction, twice as long. Those two arrows did
not turn at all. The machine that bends everything else leaves these two lines exactly where they
are and slides points along them.

An arrow that survives like this is an **eigenvector** of $A$. The number it gets multiplied by
is its **eigenvalue**, written $\lambda$. The word eigen is German for own, so these are the
matrix's own directions. One line holds the whole subject:

$$
Ax = \lambda x, \qquad x \neq 0 .
$$

The condition $x \neq 0$ is not fussiness. The zero vector satisfies $A0 = \lambda 0$ for every
number $\lambda$, so allowing it would make the definition say nothing. Also, if $x$ is an
eigenvector then so is $2x$ and so is $-x$, with the same $\lambda$. What survives is a line
through the origin, and $x$ is just a name for a point on it.

</svg>
  Three arrows, one machine. $(-1, 1)$ is turned. $(1, 1)$ and $(1, -2)$ stay on their own dotted
  lines and only change length. Those two lines are the eigendirections, and the length ratios
  5 and 2 are the eigenvalues.
</Figure>

The eigenvalue is allowed to be zero. If $Ax = 0$ for some nonzero $x$, then $x$ is an eigenvector
with $\lambda = 0$, and $x$ sits in the null space $N(A)$. You have already met one. The ring road
machine from Lecture 2 sends $(1, 1, 1)$ to $(0, 0, 0)$, because raising all three cities by a
meter changes no climb. That blind direction is an eigenvector with eigenvalue zero. Singular and
"has eigenvalue zero" are the same news said twice.

<Callout type="intuition">
  Do not start by solving equations. Start by looking for lines through the origin that the matrix
  maps back onto themselves. Every such line gives you one eigenvector and one number. The
  equations come later, and only to find the lines you could not guess.
</Callout>

<EigenExplorer />

## How to find them

Guessing works for small matrices and stops working fast. Here is the procedure, and it comes
straight out of Lecture 14.

Move everything to one side of $Ax = \lambda x$. You cannot subtract the number $\lambda$ from the
matrix $A$, so put it on the diagonal first, as $\lambda I$:

$$
(A - \lambda I)\,x = 0 .
$$

Read that as a question about one matrix. We want a nonzero $x$ in the null space of
$A - \lambda I$. A square matrix has a nonzero null vector exactly when it is singular, and
Lecture 14 gave a one-number test for singular. So $\lambda$ is an eigenvalue of $A$ precisely
when

$$
\det(A - \lambda I) = 0 .
$$

In words: the eigenvalues of $A$ are the numbers you can subtract from its diagonal to make it
singular. That one sentence is the whole method.

Run it on the matrix from Figure 1:

$$
\det \begin{bmatrix} 4 - \lambda & 1 \\ 2 & 3 - \lambda \end{bmatrix}
= (4 - \lambda)(3 - \lambda) - 2 = \lambda^2 - 7\lambda + 10 = (\lambda - 5)(\lambda - 2).
$$

The roots are $\lambda = 5$ and $\lambda = 2$, which is what Figure 1 showed. Now put each root
back and solve. For $\lambda = 5$,

$$
A - 5I = \begin{bmatrix} -1 & 1 \\ 2 & -2 \end{bmatrix},
$$

whose null space is spanned by $(1, 1)$. For $\lambda = 2$ the matrix is
$\begin{bmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 2 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ and the null space is spanned by $(1, -2)$. Two
lines, two numbers, done.

<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 3</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(10 150) rotate(0 70.4 10)" data-s="20">
<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">det(A − 3I) = −2</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(18 175) rotate(0 50.05000000000001 8.125)" data-s="21">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">still has area</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(216 130) rotate(0 19.25 8.75)" data-s="22">
<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 5</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(180 150) rotate(0 66 10)" data-s="23">
<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">det(A − 5I) = 0</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(172 175) rotate(0 71.5 8.125)" data-s="24">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">squashed onto a line</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(439 130) rotate(0 19.25 8.75)" data-s="25">
<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 2</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(403 150) rotate(0 66 10)" data-s="26">
<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">det(A − 2I) = 0</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(395 175) rotate(0 71.5 8.125)" data-s="27">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">squashed onto a line</text>
</g>
</svg>
  What $\det(A - \lambda I) = 0$ looks like. The dashed square is the unit square and the blue
  shape is where $A - \lambda I$ sends it. For a $\lambda$ that is not an eigenvalue the landing
  spot is a parallelogram, and the shaded patch is the area it still has. At $\lambda = 5$ and
  $\lambda = 2$ the plane collapses onto a line: no area, determinant zero, and some nonzero
  vector sent to the origin. That vector is the eigenvector.
</Figure>

Look again at the polynomial $\lambda^2 - 7\lambda + 10$. The 7 is $4 + 3$, the sum along the
diagonal of $A$. The 10 is $12 - 2$, the determinant of $A$. That is not a coincidence of this
example. For any $n$ by $n$ matrix,

$$
\lambda_1 + \lambda_2 + \dots + \lambda_n = \mathrm{tr}(A),
\qquad
\lambda_1 \lambda_2 \cdots \lambda_n = \det(A),
$$

where $\mathrm{tr}(A)$ is the sum of the diagonal entries, the trace. Both checks cost seconds and
both catch arithmetic slips. Here $5 + 2 = 7$ and $5 \times 2 = 10$.

One more free result. If $A$ is triangular then $A - \lambda I$ is triangular too, and the
determinant of a triangular matrix is the product of its diagonal entries. So
$\det(A - \lambda I) = (a_{11} - \lambda)\cdots(a_{nn} - \lambda)$, and the eigenvalues of a
triangular matrix are exactly the numbers sitting on its diagonal. Nothing to compute.

<Callout type="warning">
  Pivots are not eigenvalues. Elimination changes the eigenvalues, so you cannot read them off
  $U$. Take $A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 2 & 6 \end{bmatrix}$. Elimination gives
  $U = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 3 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$, whose diagonal reads 1 and 0. The real
  eigenvalues are 0 and 7, since the trace is 7 and the determinant is 0. The two lists happen to
  share a zero and nothing else. A connection does survive for symmetric matrices, and it shows up
  later in this lecture. It compares signs only.
</Callout>

## A machine that settles down

You can find eigenvalues now. Here is why anyone bothers. Two towns swap residents every year. Of the
people in town 1, eighty percent stay and twenty percent move to town 2. Of the people in town 2,
seventy percent stay and thirty percent move to town 1. Write the fractions as columns:

$$
A = \begin{bmatrix} 0.8 & 0.3 \\ 0.2 & 0.7 \end{bmatrix}.
$$

Each column sums to 1, because everybody ends up somewhere. A matrix like this is called a Markov
matrix. If $u_k$ holds the two population fractions in year $k$, then $u_{k+1} = A u_k$, and the
population in year 100 is $A^{100} u_0$. Nobody wants to multiply a hundred matrices.

Do the eigenvalue computation instead:

$$
\det \begin{bmatrix} 0.8 - \lambda & 0.3 \\ 0.2 & 0.7 - \lambda \end{bmatrix}
= \lambda^2 - 1.5\lambda + 0.5 = (\lambda - 1)(\lambda - 0.5).
$$

The trace check gives $1 + 0.5 = 1.5$ and the determinant check gives $1 \times 0.5 = 0.5$. Both
agree. For $\lambda = 1$, the matrix $A - I$ has rows $(-0.2, 0.3)$ and $(0.2, -0.3)$, so the null
space is spanned by $(3, 2)$, which we rescale to $x_1 = (0.6, 0.4)$ so the entries sum to 1. For
$\lambda = 0.5$ the null space of $A - 0.5I$ is spanned by $x_2 = (1, -1)$.

Those two numbers say everything. Start from $u_0 = (1, 0)$, everyone in town 1, and split it
along the eigenvectors:

$$
u_0 = (0.6,\, 0.4) + 0.4\,(1,\, -1).
$$

Each year, $A$ leaves the first piece alone and halves the second, because that is what
eigenvalues 1 and 0.5 do. So

$$
u_k = (0.6,\, 0.4) + 0.4 \left(\tfrac{1}{2}\right)^{k} (1,\, -1),
$$

and the second piece dies out. The population settles at 60 percent and 40 percent no matter
where it starts. The eigenvector for $\lambda = 1$ is the steady state, and the eigenvector for
$\lambda = 0.5$ is the part that decays.

</svg>
  Every column of $A$ sums to 1, so the total population never changes and every state stays on
  the line $x + y = 1$. Along that line the gap to $(0.6, 0.4)$ is multiplied by $0.5$ each year.
</Figure>

The code below runs the whole example. It checks the two eigenvectors by hand, checks the trace
and the determinant, and then computes $A^{100}$ twice: once by a hundred honest multiplications,
once from the eigenvalues alone.

<CodeTabs>
  <Fragment slot="numpy">
```python
import numpy as np

A = np.array([[0.8, 0.3], [0.2, 0.7]])

x1 = np.array([0.6, 0.4])       # null space of A - I
x2 = np.array([1.0, -1.0])      # null space of A - 0.5 I
print(A @ x1)                   # [0.6 0.4]   unchanged, so lambda = 1
print(A @ x2)                   # [ 0.5 -0.5] halved,    so lambda = 0.5

print(np.trace(A), np.linalg.det(A))   # 1.5 0.5  = 1 + 0.5 and 1 * 0.5

def power(M, k):                # the expensive way
    P = M
    for _ in range(k - 1):
        P = P @ M
    return P

print(power(A, 100))            # [[0.6 0.6]
                                #  [0.4 0.4]]

X = np.column_stack([x1, x2])   # eigenvectors in the columns
L = np.diag([1.0 ** 100, 0.5 ** 100])
print(X @ L @ np.linalg.inv(X)) # [[0.6 0.6]
                                #  [0.4 0.4]]   same answer, two numbers of work
```
  </Fragment>
  <Fragment slot="pytorch">
```python
import torch

A = torch.tensor([[0.8, 0.3], [0.2, 0.7]])

x1 = torch.tensor([0.6, 0.4])   # null space of A - I
x2 = torch.tensor([1.0, -1.0])  # null space of A - 0.5 I
print(A @ x1)                   # tensor([0.6000, 0.4000])  lambda = 1
print(A @ x2)                   # tensor([ 0.5000, -0.5000]) lambda = 0.5

print(torch.trace(A), torch.linalg.det(A))   # 1.5 0.5

def power(M, k):
    P = M
    for _ in range(k - 1):
        P = P @ M
    return P

print(power(A, 100))            # [[0.6 0.6]
                                #  [0.4 0.4]]

X = torch.stack([x1, x2], dim=1)   # torch spells column_stack as stack(dim=1)
L = torch.diag(torch.tensor([1.0 ** 100, 0.5 ** 100]))
print(X @ L @ torch.linalg.inv(X)) # [[0.6 0.6]
                                   #  [0.4 0.4]]
```
  </Fragment>

```python
import jax.numpy as jnp

A = jnp.array([[0.8, 0.3], [0.2, 0.7]])

x1 = jnp.array([0.6, 0.4])      # null space of A - I
x2 = jnp.array([1.0, -1.0])     # null space of A - 0.5 I
print(A @ x1)                   # [0.6 0.4]   unchanged, so lambda = 1
print(A @ x2)                   # [ 0.5 -0.5] halved,    so lambda = 0.5

print(jnp.trace(A), jnp.linalg.det(A))   # 1.5 0.5

def power(M, k):
    P = M
    for _ in range(k - 1):
        P = P @ M
    return P

print(power(A, 100))            # [[0.6 0.6]
                                #  [0.4 0.4]]

X = jnp.column_stack([x1, x2])
L = jnp.diag(jnp.array([1.0 ** 100, 0.5 ** 100]))
print(X @ L @ jnp.linalg.inv(X)) # [[0.6 0.6]
                                 #  [0.4 0.4]]
```
  </Fragment>
  <Fragment slot="tensorflow">
```python
import tensorflow as tf

A = tf.constant([[0.8, 0.3], [0.2, 0.7]])

x1 = tf.constant([0.6, 0.4])    # null space of A - I
x2 = tf.constant([1.0, -1.0])   # null space of A - 0.5 I
print(tf.linalg.matvec(A, x1))  # [0.6 0.4]   unchanged, so lambda = 1
print(tf.linalg.matvec(A, x2))  # [ 0.5 -0.5] halved,    so lambda = 0.5

print(tf.linalg.trace(A), tf.linalg.det(A))   # 1.5 0.5

def power(M, k):
    P = M
    for _ in range(k - 1):
        P = P @ M
    return P

print(power(A, 100))            # [[0.6 0.6]
                                #  [0.4 0.4]]

X = tf.stack([x1, x2], axis=1)  # tensorflow has no column_stack
L = tf.linalg.diag(tf.constant([1.0 ** 100, 0.5 ** 100]))
print(X @ L @ tf.linalg.inv(X)) # [[0.6 0.6]
                                #  [0.4 0.4]]
```
  </Fragment>
</CodeTabs>

Both columns of $A^{100}$ are the steady state. That is the promise of eigenvalues: a hundred
matrix multiplications collapse into raising two numbers to the hundredth power, and one of those
numbers is so small that it vanishes.

## Matrices whose eigenvalues you can read off

Some machines wear their answers on the outside. Four of them are worth memorizing, because they
show up inside everything else.

Start with the **identity**. $Ix = x$ for every $x$, so every vector is an eigenvector and the
only eigenvalue is 1. Nothing turns because nothing moves.

Next a **projection**. Lecture 11 built $P = aa\T / a\T a$, the machine that drops every vector onto
the line through $a$. Take $a = (1, 1)$, so

$$
P = \begin{bmatrix} 0.5 & 0.5 \\ 0.5 & 0.5 \end{bmatrix}.
$$

A vector already on the line, like $(1, 1)$, is left alone: $\lambda = 1$. A vector perpendicular
to the line, like $(1, -1)$, is crushed to zero: $\lambda = 0$. There is a reason those are the
only two options. Projecting twice is the same as projecting once, so $P^2 = P$, and applying
that to an eigenvector gives $\lambda^2 = \lambda$. The only numbers that survive are 0 and 1.
The column space of $P$ is the eigenvectors for $\lambda = 1$, and the null space is the
eigenvectors for $\lambda = 0$.

Double a projection and subtract the identity and you get a **reflection**, a mirror,
$R = 2P - I = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$. Vectors on the mirror line are fixed,
so $\lambda = 1$. Vectors perpendicular to it are sent to their opposites, so $\lambda = -1$.
Reflecting twice does nothing, so $R^2 = I$ and $\lambda^2 = 1$. The same argument as before, one
letter different.

Last, a **rotation**. Turn the plane a quarter circle,
$Q = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & -1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$. Every direction moves. No line survives, and
the algebra agrees: $\det(Q - \lambda I) = \lambda^2 + 1$, which no real number solves.

</svg>
  Three machines and their surviving lines. The projection keeps one line and destroys the
  perpendicular one. The reflection keeps one and reverses the other. The rotation keeps none, and
  its characteristic equation $\lambda^2 + 1 = 0$ says so.
</Figure>

<Callout type="note">
  A rotation does have eigenvalues, just not real ones: $\lambda = \pm i$ for the quarter turn.
  Notice the checks still work, since $i + (-i) = 0$ is the trace and $i \times (-i) = 1$ is the
  determinant. Complex eigenvalues are the algebra's way of saying "this motion is a rotation",
  and they are how oscillations enter differential equations. This course stays in $\R^n$, so we
  record the fact and move on.
</Callout>

Two shortcuts follow from the definition and cost nothing. Apply $A$ twice to an eigenvector and
you get $A^2 x = \lambda^2 x$, so squaring a matrix squares its eigenvalues and keeps its
eigenvectors. Shift the matrix by $cI$ and every eigenvalue shifts by $c$, since
$(A + cI)x = (\lambda + c)x$. The eigenvectors do not move at all.

The shortcut you might expect next is false. Eigenvalues of $A + B$ are not the sums of the
eigenvalues, and eigenvalues of $AB$ are not the products, because $A$ and $B$ generally have
different eigenvectors. Take $A = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ and
$B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$. Every eigenvalue of each is 0. Yet
$A + B = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ has eigenvalues $1$ and $-1$, and
$AB = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ has eigenvalues 1 and 0. Sums and products of
eigenvalues only behave when the two matrices share eigenvectors, which happens exactly when
$AB = BA$.

## Diagonalization

So far the eigenvectors have been handled one at a time. Take them all at once and the matrix
comes apart into numbers.

Suppose an $n$ by $n$ matrix has $n$ independent eigenvectors. Put them in the columns of a matrix
$X$ and put the eigenvalues on the diagonal of $\Lambda$. Then look at what $AX$ is, column by
column: the $k$-th column of $AX$ is $Ax_k$, which is $\lambda_k x_k$. That is the $k$-th column
of $X$ scaled, which is exactly the $k$-th column of $X\Lambda$. So

$$
AX = X\Lambda, \qquad A = X \Lambda X^{-1}, \qquad \Lambda = X^{-1} A X .
$$

$X$ is invertible because its columns are independent, which is the only thing we assumed.

Read the middle formula from right to left and it becomes a sentence. $X^{-1}$ rewrites a vector
in eigen-coordinates, saying how much of each eigenvector it contains. $\Lambda$ scales each of
those amounts by its own eigenvalue. $X$ writes the result back in ordinary coordinates. Change
the language, do something trivial, change the language back.

Watch it on the matrix from Figure 1, whose eigenvectors are $x_1 = (1, 1)$ with $\lambda_1 = 5$
and $x_2 = (1, -2)$ with $\lambda_2 = 2$. Take $u = (3, 0)$. In eigen-coordinates,
$u = 2x_1 + 1x_2$, since $2(1,1) + (1,-2) = (3, 0)$. Now apply $A$ by scaling each amount:

$$
Au = 2 \cdot 5\, x_1 + 1 \cdot 2\, x_2 = 10(1, 1) + 2(1, -2) = (12, 6),
$$

which is what $A(3,0)$ gives directly. The matrix never had to be multiplied.

</svg>
  Diagonalization, drawn. Split $u$ along the two eigenlines, stretch each piece by its own
  eigenvalue, add the pieces back. The parallelogram before and the parallelogram after have the
  same two sides, scaled by 5 and by 2.
</Figure>

Powers come out of this for free. Two copies give
$A^2 = X\Lambda X^{-1} X \Lambda X^{-1} = X \Lambda^2 X^{-1}$, since the inner pair cancels, and
the same cancellation happens all the way up:

$$
A^k = X \Lambda^k X^{-1} .
$$

Raising a diagonal matrix to the hundredth power means raising $n$ numbers to the hundredth power.
That is the entire trick behind the Markov example, and it also answers the stability question at
a glance: $A^k$ goes to the zero matrix exactly when every $|\lambda_i| < 1$.

It does not always work. Diagonalization needs $n$ independent eigenvectors, and some matrices do
not have them. Take

$$
A = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 0 & 1 \end{bmatrix}.
$$

It is triangular, so the eigenvalues are 1 and 1. The root $\lambda = 1$ appears twice in the
characteristic polynomial, which we call its algebraic multiplicity. But
$A - I = \begin{bmatrix} 0 & 1 \\ 0 & 0 \end{bmatrix}$ has rank 1, so its null space is only one
dimensional: there is a single eigendirection, $(1, 0)$, and no second one. The number of
independent eigenvectors for a given $\lambda$ is its geometric multiplicity, and it is never
larger than the algebraic multiplicity. When it comes up short there is no eigenvector basis and
no diagonalization. Distinct eigenvalues always give independent eigenvectors,
so the trouble can only start when a root repeats.

## Fibonacci and the golden ratio

Now a matrix where everything works, and where the answer turns out to be famous.

The numbers $0, 1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, \dots$ obey $F_{k+2} = F_{k+1} + F_k$. That is a rule for
getting the next number from the previous two, which sounds like nothing to do with matrices until
you stack two consecutive terms into a vector. Let $u_k = (F_{k+1}, F_k)$. Then

$$
u_{k+1} = \begin{bmatrix} F_{k+2} \\ F_{k+1} \end{bmatrix}
= \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 0 \end{bmatrix}
  \begin{bmatrix} F_{k+1} \\ F_{k} \end{bmatrix} = A u_k .
$$

The top row adds the two entries, the bottom row remembers the old top. So $u_k = A^k u_0$ with
$u_0 = (1, 0)$, and the Fibonacci numbers are the powers of one small matrix. Now diagonalize it:

$$
\det \begin{bmatrix} 1 - \lambda & 1 \\ 1 & -\lambda \end{bmatrix}
= \lambda^2 - \lambda - 1 = 0
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\lambda = \frac{1 \pm \sqrt{5}}{2}.
$$

The larger root is the golden ratio $\varphi \approx 1.618$, and the smaller is
$\psi \approx -0.618$. The trace check gives $\varphi + \psi = 1$ and the determinant check gives
$\varphi \psi = -1$, both of which you can read straight off the polynomial. For the eigenvectors,
the second row of $A - \lambda I$ says $x_1 = \lambda x_2$, so the eigenvector is $(\lambda, 1)$.

Split $u_0 = (1, 0)$ along them. Solving $c_1(\varphi, 1) + c_2(\psi, 1) = (1, 0)$ gives
$c_2 = -c_1$ and $c_1(\varphi - \psi) = 1$, and since $\varphi - \psi = \sqrt{5}$ we get
$c_1 = 1/\sqrt5$. Multiply by $A$ a hundred times, which now means multiplying each piece by its
own eigenvalue a hundred times, and read the bottom entry:

$$
F_k = \frac{\varphi^{\,k} - \psi^{\,k}}{\sqrt 5}.
$$

A sequence defined by addition has a closed form built from square roots. And because
$|\psi| < 1$, the second term shrinks to nothing: $F_k$ is the nearest integer to
$\varphi^k / \sqrt5$. It also explains a fact people notice without knowing why. The ratio
$F_{k+1}/F_k$ marches toward $\varphi$, because the $\varphi$ piece grows and the $\psi$ piece
dies, so after a while the vector $u_k$ is pointing almost exactly along the eigenvector
$(\varphi, 1)$.

<g transform="translate(250 249) rotate(0 71.5 8.125)" data-s="19">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">φ every quarter turn</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The squares are Fibonacci numbers and the arcs join into a spiral. The eigenvalue $\varphi$ is
  the growth factor: each square's side is $\varphi$ times the one before it in the limit, so each
  quarter turn of the spiral grows by very nearly $\varphi$.
</Figure>

<CodeTabs>
  <Fragment slot="numpy">
```python
import numpy as np

phi = (1 + 5 ** 0.5) / 2
psi = (1 - 5 ** 0.5) / 2
print(phi, psi)            # 1.618033988749895 -0.6180339887498949
print(phi + psi, phi * psi)  # 1.0 -1.0000000000000002  the trace and the determinant

def fib_closed(n):
    return (phi ** n - psi ** n) / 5 ** 0.5

def fib_loop(n):
    a, b = 0, 1
    for _ in range(n):
        a, b = b, a + b
    return a

for n in [10, 30, 50]:
    print(n, fib_loop(n), fib_closed(n))
# 10 55 55.000000000000014
# 30 832040 832040.0000000008
# 50 12586269025 12586269025.00002

A = np.array([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 0.0]])
u = np.array([1.0, 0.0])
for _ in range(10):
    u = A @ u
print(u)                     # [89. 55.]  which is (F11, F10)
print(fib_loop(101) / fib_loop(100))   # 1.618033988749895
```
  </Fragment>
  <Fragment slot="pytorch">
```python
import torch

phi = (1 + 5 ** 0.5) / 2
psi = (1 - 5 ** 0.5) / 2
print(phi, psi)            # 1.618033988749895 -0.6180339887498949
print(phi + psi, phi * psi)  # 1.0 -1.0000000000000002  the trace and the determinant

def fib_closed(n):
    return (phi ** n - psi ** n) / 5 ** 0.5

def fib_loop(n):
    a, b = 0, 1
    for _ in range(n):
        a, b = b, a + b
    return a

for n in [10, 30, 50]:
    print(n, fib_loop(n), fib_closed(n))
# 10 55 55.000000000000014
# 30 832040 832040.0000000008
# 50 12586269025 12586269025.00002

A = torch.tensor([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 0.0]], dtype=torch.float64)
u = torch.tensor([1.0, 0.0], dtype=torch.float64)
for _ in range(10):
    u = A @ u
print(u)                     # tensor([89., 55.])  which is (F11, F10)
print(fib_loop(101) / fib_loop(100))   # 1.618033988749895
```

```python
print(phi + psi, phi * psi)  # 1.0 -1.0000000000000002  the trace and the determinant

def fib_closed(n):

    return a

# 10 55 55.000000000000014
# 30 832040 832040.0000000008
# 50 12586269025 12586269025.00002
```

  <Fragment slot="tensorflow">
```python
import tensorflow as tf

phi = (1 + 5 ** 0.5) / 2
psi = (1 - 5 ** 0.5) / 2
print(phi, psi)            # 1.618033988749895 -0.6180339887498949
print(phi + psi, phi * psi)  # 1.0 -1.0000000000000002  the trace and the determinant

def fib_closed(n):
    return (phi ** n - psi ** n) / 5 ** 0.5

def fib_loop(n):
    a, b = 0, 1
    for _ in range(n):
        a, b = b, a + b
    return a

for n in [10, 30, 50]:
    print(n, fib_loop(n), fib_closed(n))
# 10 55 55.000000000000014
# 30 832040 832040.0000000008
# 50 12586269025 12586269025.00002

A = tf.constant([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 0.0]], dtype=tf.float64)
u = tf.constant([1.0, 0.0], dtype=tf.float64)
for _ in range(10):
    u = tf.linalg.matvec(A, u)
print(u)                     # [89. 55.]  which is (F11, F10)
print(fib_loop(101) / fib_loop(100))   # 1.618033988749895
```
  </Fragment>
</CodeTabs>

The closed form drifts from the integers in the sixteenth significant digit, which is a double
doing its best, and the loop stays exact. Both are worth having. The formula tells you the answer
grows like $\varphi^k$; the loop tells you the answer.

That last observation, that repeated multiplication drags any starting vector toward the largest
eigenvalue's direction, is an algorithm. It is called power iteration, and it is how the biggest
eigenvalue of a huge matrix gets computed when nobody can afford a characteristic polynomial.
Press the button and watch it happen.

<PowerStepper />

## Symmetric matrices are the good case

Everything so far has worked for any matrix with enough eigenvectors. One family does much
better than that, and it happens to be the family your data arrives in.

A matrix is symmetric when $S = S\T$, so the entry in row $i$ column $j$ equals the entry in row
$j$ column $i$. This is not a rare accident. Covariance matrices are symmetric. So is every Gram
matrix $A\T A$, and so is the second-derivative matrix of any smooth function. Symmetric matrices
come with two gifts.

The first gift is that **the eigenvalues are real**. No rotations hide inside a symmetric matrix,
so the complex case never arises.

The second is that **the eigenvectors are perpendicular**. Here is the whole proof for two
different eigenvalues.
Suppose $Sx_1 = \lambda x_1$ and $Sx_2 = \mu x_2$ with $\lambda \neq \mu$. Then

$$
\lambda \, (x_1 \cdot x_2) = (Sx_1) \cdot x_2 = x_1 \cdot (Sx_2) = \mu \, (x_1 \cdot x_2),
$$

where the middle step is where symmetry is used, since $(Sx_1)\T x_2 = x_1\T S\T x_2$ and
$S\T = S$. So $(\lambda - \mu)(x_1 \cdot x_2) = 0$, and since $\lambda \neq \mu$ the dot product
must be zero. Perpendicular.

Scale each eigenvector to length 1 and call them $q_1, \dots, q_n$. Put them in the columns of
$Q$. Perpendicular unit columns mean $Q\T Q = I$, so $Q^{-1} = Q\T$, and diagonalization loses its
inverse:

$$
S = Q \Lambda Q\T .
$$

This is the spectral theorem, and it holds for every symmetric matrix, even when eigenvalues
repeat. Read the three factors right to left: $Q\T$ rotates space so the eigenvectors become the
coordinate axes, $\Lambda$ stretches along those axes, $Q$ rotates back. Every symmetric matrix is
a stretch in disguise, and the disguise is a rotation.

Take $S = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{bmatrix}$. The trace is 4 and the determinant is 3,
so the eigenvalues are 3 and 1. The eigenvectors are $q_1 = (1,1)/\sqrt2$ and
$q_2 = (1,-1)/\sqrt2$, which are perpendicular as promised. Feed $S$ the unit circle and it comes
out as an ellipse with half-axes 3 and 1, pointing along $q_1$ and $q_2$.

<g transform="translate(10 87) rotate(0 75.075 8.125)" data-s="11">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">its image, an ellipse</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The symmetric matrix with rows $(2,1)$ and $(1,2)$ turns the unit circle into an ellipse. The
  axes of that ellipse are the eigenvectors and their half-lengths are the eigenvalues. Rotate,
  stretch, rotate back.
</Figure>

There is one more way to read $S = Q\Lambda Q\T$, and it is the one that will matter later.
Multiply it out as a sum of columns times rows:

$$
S = \lambda_1 q_1 q_1\T + \lambda_2 q_2 q_2\T + \dots + \lambda_n q_n q_n\T .
$$

Every piece $q_i q_i\T$ is a familiar object. It is Lecture 11's projection matrix
$aa\T / a\T a$ with $a = q_i$, and since $q_i$ has length 1 the denominator is 1. So each piece
projects onto one eigendirection, and $S$ scales that projection by $\lambda_i$ and adds the
results. A symmetric matrix is a weighted sum of projections onto perpendicular lines. For the
example, $q_1 q_1\T = \tfrac12\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ and
$q_2 q_2\T = \tfrac12\begin{bmatrix} 1 & -1 \\ -1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$, and $3$ times the first plus
$1$ times the second rebuilds $\begin{bmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 1 & 2 \end{bmatrix}$ exactly.

## Positive definite

Some symmetric matrices have all their eigenvalues on the positive side. That single condition
turns out to be the difference between a surface with a bottom and a surface without one, which is
why this section is really about optimization.

A symmetric matrix $S$ is **positive definite** when the number

$$
x\T S x
$$

is greater than zero for every nonzero $x$. That number is called the energy. For a 2 by 2 matrix
with rows $(a, b)$ and $(b, c)$ the energy works out to $a x_1^2 + 2b x_1 x_2 + c x_2^2$. So the
condition says: this quadratic is positive everywhere except at the origin.

Why is that the same as "all eigenvalues positive"? Because of the spectral theorem. Write $x$ in
the eigenvector basis, $x = c_1 q_1 + \dots + c_n q_n$. The $q_i$ are perpendicular unit vectors,
so the cross terms vanish and

$$
x\T S x = \lambda_1 c_1^2 + \lambda_2 c_2^2 + \dots + \lambda_n c_n^2 .
$$

The energy is a weighted sum of squares with the eigenvalues as weights. It is positive for every
nonzero $x$ exactly when every weight is positive. Take one $\lambda_i$ negative and the energy
goes negative along $q_i$.

For a symmetric matrix, four tests all say the same thing, and each is worth having because each
is cheap in a different situation:

- every eigenvalue is positive;
- every pivot from elimination is positive;
- every leading top-left determinant is positive, the $1\times1$, the $2\times2$, and so on;
- $S = A\T A$ for some matrix $A$ with independent columns.

The last one is the reason positive definite matrices are everywhere in machine learning. If $A$
holds your data, then $x\T A\T A x = (Ax)\T(Ax) = \norm{Ax}^2$, which is a squared length and so
never negative, and it is zero only when $Ax = 0$, which independent columns forbid. Least squares
and covariance both produce $A\T A$, so both produce positive definite matrices whenever the data
has no redundant column.

Try all four on the tridiagonal matrix from Lecture 5:

$$
K = \begin{bmatrix} 2 & -1 & 0 \\ -1 & 2 & -1 \\ 0 & -1 & 2 \end{bmatrix}.
$$

Its pivots are $2, \tfrac32, \tfrac43$, all positive. Its leading determinants are $2, 3, 4$, all
positive. Its eigenvalues are $2 - \sqrt2 \approx 0.586$, then $2$, then $2 + \sqrt2 \approx 3.414$,
all positive, and they multiply to 4, which is the determinant, and sum to 6, which is the trace.
And Cholesky factors it as $K = LL\T$, which is the fourth test with $A = L\T$. Four tests, one
verdict.

**Positive semidefinite** relaxes every strict inequality to an inclusive one. The energy is
allowed to reach zero: $x\T S x \geq 0$ for every $x$, some eigenvalue may be 0, some pivot may be
0, and the determinant may be 0. The two names get swapped constantly, so keep the distinction
sharp: definite means the bowl has exactly one lowest point, semidefinite means the bowl may have
a flat valley floor running through it.

$N = \begin{bmatrix} 1 & 1 \\ 1 & 1 \end{bmatrix}$ is the small example. Its energy is
$x_1^2 + 2x_1x_2 + x_2^2 = (x_1 + x_2)^2$, which is never negative and is zero all along the line
$x_1 + x_2 = 0$. Its eigenvalues are 2 and 0. Semidefinite, not definite. And
$\begin{bmatrix} 1 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}$, with eigenvalues 1 and $-1$, has energy
$x_1^2 - x_2^2$, which is positive in one direction and negative in another. That one is called
indefinite, and its graph is a saddle.

<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">indefinite</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(233 228) rotate(0 71.5 8.125)" data-s="48">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 1, −1. A saddle.</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(430 207) rotate(0 80.85000000000002 8.75)" data-s="49">
<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">positive semidefinite</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(427 228) rotate(0 85.80000000000001 8.125)" data-s="50">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 2, 0. A flat valley.</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The graph of the energy $x\T S x$, for three symmetric matrices. Positive eigenvalues curve the
  surface up in every direction. A negative one opens a downhill escape. A zero one flattens a
  whole line, and the bottom stretches into a valley. That flat direction is a null direction of
  $S$, which is why the trough carries the null-space color from the four-subspaces picture.
</Figure>

You have met this test before. In one variable you look at $f''$: positive means the curve holds
water and you are sitting at the bottom of it. The Hessian is that same number with more
directions to account for, and you read the verdict off the signs exactly as you always did.

<Figure n="9">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 671.6627818995157 267" width="672" role="img" aria-label="On the left a one-variable parabola with a positive second derivative and a minimum at the bottom. On the right the same verdict in two variables, a bowl whose Hessian has eigenvalues 1 and 9. Beneath, three sign patterns and the shape each one gives: both positive a bowl, opposite signs a saddle, one zero a valley." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:480px">

<g transform="translate(557.558984806265 234.99946192713242) rotate(0 30.799999999999983 8.75)" data-s="31">
<text x="0" y="12.334" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="14px" fill="#141414" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">a valley</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The second derivative test, grown up. On the left one number settles it. On the right that
  number becomes a list, the eigenvalues of the Hessian, and the signs decide the shape: all
  positive gives the green bowl, opposite signs the pink saddle, a zero the cyan valley. Flip
  every sign and the bowl becomes a peak.
</Figure>

<Callout type="warning">
  The leading-determinant test only works for positive definite, never for semidefinite. Take
  $\begin{bmatrix} 0 & 0 \\ 0 & -1 \end{bmatrix}$: its leading determinants are 0 and 0, both
  nonnegative, yet its energy is $-x_2^2$, which is negative. Relaxing Sylvester's test to
  "greater than or equal" does not test semidefiniteness. You need every principal minor, not only
  the top-left ones. Or check the eigenvalues.
</Callout>

<CodeTabs>
  <Fragment slot="numpy">
```python
import numpy as np

K = np.array([[2.0, -1.0, 0.0], [-1.0, 2.0, -1.0], [0.0, -1.0, 2.0]])

print(np.linalg.eigvalsh(K))    # [0.58578644 2. 3.41421356]   2 - sqrt2, 2, 2 + sqrt2

L = np.linalg.cholesky(K)       # succeeds only for positive definite input
print(np.diag(L) ** 2)          # [2. 1.5 1.33333333]  the pivots

print(np.round([np.linalg.det(K[:1, :1]),
                np.linalg.det(K[:2, :2]),
                np.linalg.det(K)], 6))            # [2. 3. 4.]  the leading determinants

for x in [np.array([1.0, 2.0, 3.0]), np.array([1.0, 1.0, 1.0])]:
    print(x @ K @ x)            # 12.0 then 2.0   energy, positive away from the origin

lam = np.linalg.eigvalsh(K)
Q = np.linalg.eigh(K)[1]
S = sum(lam[i] * np.outer(Q[:, i], Q[:, i]) for i in range(3))
print(np.round(S, 10))          # rebuilds K from rank-one projections

N = np.array([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 1.0]])
print(np.linalg.eigvalsh(N))          # [0. 2.]   semidefinite, not definite
d = np.array([1.0, -1.0])
print(d @ N @ d)                      # 0.0   the flat direction
```
  </Fragment>

```python
import torch

K = torch.tensor([[2.0, -1.0, 0.0], [-1.0, 2.0, -1.0], [0.0, -1.0, 2.0]], dtype=torch.float64)

print(torch.linalg.eigvalsh(K))  # [0.58578644 2. 3.41421356]  2 - sqrt2, 2, 2 + sqrt2

L = torch.linalg.cholesky(K)     # raises only for non positive definite input
print(torch.diag(L) ** 2)        # [2. 1.5 1.33333333]  the pivots

print(torch.round(torch.stack([torch.linalg.det(K[:1, :1]),
                               torch.linalg.det(K[:2, :2]),
                               torch.linalg.det(K)]), decimals=6))   # [2. 3. 4.]

for x in [torch.tensor([1.0, 2.0, 3.0], dtype=torch.float64),
          torch.tensor([1.0, 1.0, 1.0], dtype=torch.float64)]:
    print(x @ K @ x)             # 12.0 then 2.0   energy, positive away from the origin

lam, Q = torch.linalg.eigh(K)
S = sum(lam[i] * torch.outer(Q[:, i], Q[:, i]) for i in range(3))
print(torch.round(S, decimals=10))    # rebuilds K from rank-one projections

N = torch.tensor([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 1.0]], dtype=torch.float64)
print(torch.linalg.eigvalsh(N))       # [0. 2.]   semidefinite, not definite
d = torch.tensor([1.0, -1.0], dtype=torch.float64)
print(d @ N @ d)                      # 0.0   the flat direction
```

```python
import jax.numpy as jnp
from jax import config
config.update("jax_enable_x64", True)

K = jnp.array([[2.0, -1.0, 0.0], [-1.0, 2.0, -1.0], [0.0, -1.0, 2.0]])

print(jnp.linalg.eigvalsh(K))   # [0.58578644 2. 3.41421356]  2 - sqrt2, 2, 2 + sqrt2

L = jnp.linalg.cholesky(K)      # returns nan entries for non positive definite input
print(jnp.diag(L) ** 2)         # [2. 1.5 1.33333333]  the pivots

print(jnp.round(jnp.array([jnp.linalg.det(K[:1, :1]),
                           jnp.linalg.det(K[:2, :2]),
                           jnp.linalg.det(K)]), 6))       # [2. 3. 4.]

for x in [jnp.array([1.0, 2.0, 3.0]), jnp.array([1.0, 1.0, 1.0])]:
    print(x @ K @ x)            # 12.0 then 2.0   energy, positive away from the origin

lam, Q = jnp.linalg.eigh(K)
S = sum(lam[i] * jnp.outer(Q[:, i], Q[:, i]) for i in range(3))
print(jnp.round(S, 10))         # rebuilds K from rank-one projections

N = jnp.array([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 1.0]])
print(jnp.linalg.eigvalsh(N))         # [0. 2.]   semidefinite, not definite
d = jnp.array([1.0, -1.0])
print(d @ N @ d)                      # 0.0   the flat direction
```

  <Fragment slot="tensorflow">
```python
import tensorflow as tf

K = tf.constant([[2.0, -1.0, 0.0], [-1.0, 2.0, -1.0], [0.0, -1.0, 2.0]], dtype=tf.float64)

print(tf.linalg.eigvalsh(K))    # [0.58578644 2. 3.41421356]  2 - sqrt2, 2, 2 + sqrt2

L = tf.linalg.cholesky(K)       # fills with nan for non positive definite input
print(tf.linalg.diag_part(L) ** 2)   # [2. 1.5 1.33333333]  the pivots
                                     # tensorflow splits diag into diag and diag_part

print(tf.round(tf.stack([tf.linalg.det(K[:1, :1]),
                         tf.linalg.det(K[:2, :2]),
                         tf.linalg.det(K)]) * 1e6) / 1e6)   # [2. 3. 4.]

for x in [tf.constant([1.0, 2.0, 3.0], dtype=tf.float64),
          tf.constant([1.0, 1.0, 1.0], dtype=tf.float64)]:
    print(tf.tensordot(x, tf.linalg.matvec(K, x), 1))   # 12.0 then 2.0   the energy

lam, Q = tf.linalg.eigh(K)
S = sum(lam[i] * tf.tensordot(Q[:, i], Q[:, i], axes=0) for i in range(3))
print(S)                        # rebuilds K from rank-one projections
                                # tensorflow has no outer, so tensordot with axes=0

N = tf.constant([[1.0, 1.0], [1.0, 1.0]], dtype=tf.float64)
print(tf.linalg.eigvalsh(N))          # [0. 2.]   semidefinite, not definite
d = tf.constant([1.0, -1.0], dtype=tf.float64)
print(tf.tensordot(d, tf.linalg.matvec(N, d), 1))   # 0.0   the flat direction
```

</CodeTabs>

## Why gradient descent zigzags

Now the payoff. Training a model means walking downhill on a loss surface. Near a minimum, every
smooth loss looks like a quadratic, and the quadratic is written with a symmetric matrix, the
matrix of second derivatives called the Hessian. So study the simplest possible loss, with $S$
symmetric and positive definite:

$$
f(x) = \tfrac12 \, x\T S x .
$$

Its lowest point is $x = 0$ and its gradient is $Sx$. The level sets $f(x) = c$ are ellipses,
which Figure 7 already showed you: axes along the eigenvectors, and along $q_i$ the ellipse reaches
out to $\sqrt{2c/\lambda_i}$. A big eigenvalue means a tight, steeply curved direction. A small
eigenvalue means a long, nearly flat one.

Gradient descent takes the step $x \leftarrow x - \alpha S x$. Write $x$ in the eigenvector basis
again and the update falls apart into $n$ separate scalar updates:

$$
c_i \leftarrow (1 - \alpha \lambda_i)\, c_i .
$$

One number per direction, no interaction. That one line explains everything people say about
learning rates. For the method to converge you need $|1 - \alpha\lambda_i| < 1$ in every
direction, so $\alpha < 2/\lambda_{\max}$: the steepest direction sets the speed limit. But the
error along the flattest direction shrinks by $|1 - \alpha\lambda_{\min}|$, which sits close to 1
when $\lambda_{\min}$ is small. The steep direction caps your step; the flat direction decides how
many steps you take. The ratio

$$
\kappa = \frac{\lambda_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}}
$$

is the condition number, and it is the number that matters. With the best possible constant step
$\alpha = 2/(\lambda_{\min} + \lambda_{\max})$, the error shrinks by a factor
$(\kappa - 1)/(\kappa + 1)$ each step, which is close to 1 whenever $\kappa$ is large.

Two matrices make the point without changing anything else:

$$
S_1 = \begin{bmatrix} 5 & -1 \\ -1 & 5 \end{bmatrix},
\qquad
S_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 5 & -4 \\ -4 & 5 \end{bmatrix}.
$$

Both are symmetric with the same eigenvectors, $(1,1)/\sqrt2$ and $(1,-1)/\sqrt2$. Both have
trace 10, so both take the same best step $\alpha = 0.2$. The only difference is the spread:
$S_1$ has eigenvalues 4 and 6, so $\kappa = 1.5$, and $S_2$ has eigenvalues 1 and 9, so
$\kappa = 9$.

Those eigenvalues are curvatures, and that is the whole of it. Slice the bowl along an eigenvector
$q$ and you get a parabola: putting $x = t q$ into $f$ leaves $\tfrac12 \lambda t^2$, whose second
derivative is $\lambda$ itself. The eigenvalues of the Hessian are how hard the bowl bends in its
two special directions.

<Figure n="10">
  <svg slot="drawing" viewBox="0 0 481.04893533059874 203.25" width="481" role="img" aria-label="On the left the bowl of one half x transpose S2 x, with the cut along its steep eigenvector drawn in orange and the cut along its shallow eigenvector in green. On the right the same two cuts drawn flat as parabolas on one pair of axes, the orange one nine times as steep as the green one." style="max-width:100%;height:auto;min-width:440px">
<defs>
</defs>

<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 9</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(427.04893533059874 128) rotate(0 22 10)" data-s="22">
<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">λ = 1</text>

<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">f</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(425.04893533059874 152) rotate(0 4.400000000000006 10)" data-s="24">
<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">t</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(21.048935330598724 177) rotate(0 78.65 8.125)" data-s="25">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">the bowl, cut two ways</text>
</g>
<g transform="translate(253.04893533059874 177) rotate(0 100.10000000000002 8.125)" data-s="26">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">the same two cuts, laid flat</text>
</g>
</svg>
  The two eigenvalues of $S_2$, drawn as what they are. Cut the bowl along its steep eigenvector
  and the cut is the orange parabola $\tfrac12 \cdot 9\,t^2$. Cut along the shallow one and it is
  the green parabola $\tfrac12 \cdot 1\,t^2$. Both are drawn to one scale, so the nine to one you
  see is the nine to one in the eigenvalues.
</Figure>

Starting from $(1, 1.5)$ and running until the distance to the minimum is below $10^{-6}$, the
nearly round bowl takes 9 steps and the stretched one takes 65.

</svg>
  Same starting point, same step size, same eigenvector directions. Only the eigenvalue spread
  differs. On the round bowl the gradient points nearly at the minimum. On the stretched one it
  points mostly across the valley, so the path bounces from wall to wall and inches forward.
</Figure>

The picture also shows what momentum fixes. The bouncing direction reverses sign every step, since
$1 - \alpha\lambda_{\max}$ is negative there, while the crawling direction keeps the same sign.
Momentum averages the recent steps, so the reversals cancel each other and the steady ones add up.
The update becomes $v \leftarrow \beta v - \alpha S x$ then $x \leftarrow x + v$, and with tuned
$\alpha$ and $\beta$ the convergence factor improves from $(\kappa-1)/(\kappa+1)$ to about
$(\sqrt\kappa - 1)/(\sqrt\kappa + 1)$. On the stretched bowl that turns 65 steps into 25.

<CodeTabs>
  <Fragment slot="numpy">
```python
import numpy as np

round_bowl = np.array([[5.0, -1.0], [-1.0, 5.0]])   # eigenvalues 4 and 6
stretched  = np.array([[5.0, -4.0], [-4.0, 5.0]])   # eigenvalues 1 and 9
print(np.linalg.eigvalsh(round_bowl), np.linalg.eigvalsh(stretched))
# [4. 6.] [1. 9.]

def descend(S, step=0.2, beta=0.0, tol=1e-6):
    x = np.array([1.0, 1.5])
    v = np.zeros(2)
    n = 0
    while np.sqrt(x @ x) > tol:
        v = beta * v - step * (S @ x)   # beta = 0 is plain gradient descent
        x = x + v
        n += 1
    return n

print(descend(round_bowl))                        # 9
print(descend(stretched))                         # 65
print(descend(stretched, step=0.25, beta=0.25))   # 25  momentum
```

```python
import torch

round_bowl = torch.tensor([[5.0, -1.0], [-1.0, 5.0]], dtype=torch.float64)
stretched  = torch.tensor([[5.0, -4.0], [-4.0, 5.0]], dtype=torch.float64)
print(torch.linalg.eigvalsh(round_bowl), torch.linalg.eigvalsh(stretched))
# [4. 6.] [1. 9.]

def descend(S, step=0.2, beta=0.0, tol=1e-6):
    x = torch.tensor([1.0, 1.5], dtype=torch.float64)
    v = torch.zeros(2, dtype=torch.float64)
    n = 0
    while torch.sqrt(x @ x) > tol:
        v = beta * v - step * (S @ x)   # beta = 0 is plain gradient descent
        x = x + v
        n += 1
    return n

print(descend(round_bowl))                        # 9
print(descend(stretched))                         # 65
print(descend(stretched, step=0.25, beta=0.25))   # 25  momentum
```

```python
import jax.numpy as jnp
from jax import config
config.update("jax_enable_x64", True)

round_bowl = jnp.array([[5.0, -1.0], [-1.0, 5.0]])   # eigenvalues 4 and 6
stretched  = jnp.array([[5.0, -4.0], [-4.0, 5.0]])   # eigenvalues 1 and 9
print(jnp.linalg.eigvalsh(round_bowl), jnp.linalg.eigvalsh(stretched))
# [4. 6.] [1. 9.]

def descend(S, step=0.2, beta=0.0, tol=1e-6):
    x = jnp.array([1.0, 1.5])
    v = jnp.zeros(2)
    n = 0
    while jnp.sqrt(x @ x) > tol:
        v = beta * v - step * (S @ x)   # beta = 0 is plain gradient descent
        x = x + v
        n += 1
    return n

print(descend(round_bowl))                        # 9
print(descend(stretched))                         # 65
print(descend(stretched, step=0.25, beta=0.25))   # 25  momentum
```

  </Fragment>
  <Fragment slot="tensorflow">
```python
import tensorflow as tf

round_bowl = tf.constant([[5.0, -1.0], [-1.0, 5.0]], dtype=tf.float64)
stretched  = tf.constant([[5.0, -4.0], [-4.0, 5.0]], dtype=tf.float64)
print(tf.linalg.eigvalsh(round_bowl), tf.linalg.eigvalsh(stretched))
# [4. 6.] [1. 9.]

def descend(S, step=0.2, beta=0.0, tol=1e-6):
    x = tf.constant([1.0, 1.5], dtype=tf.float64)
    v = tf.zeros(2, dtype=tf.float64)
    n = 0
    while tf.sqrt(tf.tensordot(x, x, 1)) > tol:
        v = beta * v - step * tf.linalg.matvec(S, x)   # beta = 0 is plain descent
        x = x + v
        n += 1
    return n

print(descend(round_bowl))                        # 9
print(descend(stretched))                         # 65
print(descend(stretched, step=0.25, beta=0.25))   # 25  momentum
```
  </Fragment>
</CodeTabs>

Momentum treats the symptom. Here is the cure. Gradient descent knows only the slope, so for a
given steepness it takes the same step whether the valley is tight or wide. Newton's method
divides by the curvature: its step is $-H^{-1} g$, and on a quadratic bowl that lands on the
minimum from wherever you start, in one move. From $(1, 1.5)$ the gradient is $(-1, 3.5)$, so the
gradient step goes to $(1.2, 0.8)$, over on the far side of the valley floor. The Newton step is
$-H^{-1} g = (-1, -1.5)$, which lands on $(0, 0)$ exactly.

<g transform="translate(301.85407794480756 171) rotate(0 48.400000000000006 10)" data-s="13">
<text x="0" y="14.096" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="16px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">Newton step</text>

<g transform="translate(301.85407794480756 194) rotate(0 71.5 8.125)" data-s="14">
<text x="0" y="11.453" font-family="Virgil, Segoe UI Emoji" font-size="13px" fill="currentColor" text-anchor="start" style="white-space: pre;" direction="ltr" dominant-baseline="alphabetic">lands on the minimum</text>
</g>
</svg>
  One start, two steps, on the stretched bowl of Figure 11. Blue is the gradient step: it leaves
  perpendicular to the contour and crosses the valley floor to land on the far side. Green is the
  Newton step $-H^{-1} g$, which lands on the minimum. Lecture 0 ran Newton on a curve to hunt for
  a root; the tangent line has become a tangent bowl, and dividing by $f'$ has become multiplying
  by $H^{-1}$.
</Figure>

<Callout type="intuition">
  When someone says a problem is ill conditioned, picture the long ellipse. The loss has one
  direction that punishes you for stepping too far and another that barely responds at all, and a
  single learning rate has to serve both. Momentum, adaptive rates, preconditioning, normalization
  layers: they are all attempts to make the eigenvalues of the Hessian more equal, so the ellipse
  becomes rounder.
</Callout>

## Where this is going

You now have the two facts that carry the rest of the course. Any symmetric matrix is a stretch
along perpendicular axes, $S = Q\Lambda Q\T$. Any matrix of the form $A\T A$ is symmetric with
nonnegative eigenvalues.

Put them together on a data matrix. Center your data so each column has mean zero, and the
covariance matrix is $\frac{1}{n} A\T A$. It is symmetric, so it has perpendicular eigenvectors,
and it is at least positive semidefinite, so its eigenvalues are all nonnegative. Those
eigenvectors are the directions along which the data spreads out most, and the eigenvalues measure
how much spread sits in each one. That is principal component analysis, and it is the first thing
Part VI does.

The same trick applied to a matrix that is not square gives the singular value decomposition, and
with that the course finishes what it started in Lecture 1.

<Callout type="check">
  1. Find the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of $\begin{bmatrix} 3 & 1 \\ 1 & 3 \end{bmatrix}$ by
     hand. Check your answer against the trace and the determinant before reading further.
  2. $\begin{bmatrix} 2 & 0 \\ 0 & 2 \end{bmatrix}$ and
     $\begin{bmatrix} 2 & 1 \\ 0 & 2 \end{bmatrix}$ both have $\lambda = 2$ twice. How many
     independent eigenvectors does each have? Which one can be diagonalized?
  3. Start the two-town Markov chain at $u_0 = (0, 1)$ instead. Write $u_0$ in the eigenvector
     basis, predict $u_3$ on paper, then check it with the code.
  4. Is $\begin{bmatrix} 2 & 3 \\ 3 & 2 \end{bmatrix}$ positive definite? Answer with the pivots
     and again with the eigenvalues, and confirm the two signs agree.
  5. For $S_2 = \begin{bmatrix} 5 & -4 \\ -4 & 5 \end{bmatrix}$, sketch the curve
     $x\T S x = 1$. Which way does the long axis point and how long is it?
  6. Redraw Figure 11 from memory, labels included. The drawing is the test.
</Callout>
